Decisions (28)

Importers live outside the binary, as ductile plugins A source is a Python plugin in anam-importers that knows one vendor and shapes rows to a Definition; one generic anam_submit posts under a per-source token; Anam keeps the Definitions, the gate and the record and knows no vendor.

decision core basis: docs ../anam-importers/anam_importer/ductile.py#run anchor request = json.load(sys.stdin)

Alternatives

  • Capture workers compiled into the binary that owns the ledger (how health-db, withings, bp-csv, records and documents began; withings and bp-csv moved out in cards 54 and 80, records and documents in card 119, and sharing a binary let their identity composition drift)
  • Ductile holding schemas or derived data, or Anam scheduling and polling (both refused, since each puts back the coupling the split removed)

Affects Importer base (anam_importer), anam_submit, Daemon, Admission gate, Importer

Context. The gateway (ductile) decides when to do things; Anam is the record of what was learned and when. Vendor table layouts inside Anam's binary braided what a source says with how Anam stores it.

Choice. An importer reads the native format, shapes payloads only, validates locally against the Definition it carries, cuts batches of 49, and emits an event; the gateway routes it to that importer's anam_submit instance, which holds the token. The contract is docs/IMPORTERS.md §8: the wire format, tokens, all-or-nothing batches, and what each status means. What is borrowed: Confluent's immutable numbered schema versions, CloudEvents' envelope field names, Splunk HEC's tokens pinned to sourcetypes, Elastic's integrations shipping their own schemas.

Why. The gate already owned the admission contract; moving the vendor out makes the code agree with ARCHITECTURE §2's "a pluggable client, not part of the kernel", and a crashed importer can only fail to send, never corrupt the record.

What it costs. Each source needs a resend-everything mode and a cut-over (the records and documents importers kept their sources' names, so they resend what Anam's own workers admitted as no-op; the Garmin importer replaced health-db under a new source name, so the old source was retired by tombstone). The two repos version separately.

Where else it is used

  • mentioned by Admit a batch, Hand Anam a document
A distinction goes in the cheapest place that lets its reader ask cleanly A type is domain.concept[.specialisation…]; a distinction lives in the type name, the Definition's meta, a payload field, provenance, an edge or a projection, and is promoted up that list only when something forces it.

decision supporting basis: docs internal/facet/facet.go#typePattern anchor var typePattern = regexp.MustCompile(

Alternatives

  • Exactly two segments, domain.concept (card 147's sentence "Type stays domain.concept … two segments … as today"; it kept the old shape, nobody reasoned it, and the code never enforced it, so it is dropped)
  • Put every distinction in the type name (refused; each one costs a rename run and a new version of every facet, and a type per exercise or per test code multiplies without bound)
  • Put every distinction in the payload (refused; a different shape or a type that closes a series cannot be a field, because the fold reads that from the Definition)

Affects Definition manifest, A hard-coded value is a default or an invariant

Context. Card 147 closed the head of a type name at eight domains (cards 222 and 239 made it nine and then ten, and card 241 opened money from the reserved four, below) and wrote that the name stays domain.concept. The two-segment half was never argued, and facet.typePattern takes any number of dotted segments. Matt, 2026-10-21: "we control our destiny not AGENTS.md". A third or later segment is allowed, for a specialisation. That leaves the question of when a distinction earns a segment at all, and the answer was being found type by type.

Choice. A type is domain.concept[.specialisation…]. The domain is one of the closed ten, health, activity, place, record, mind, org and money in use, and social, media and work held back. A head is added only by a logged decision. A distinction goes in the cheapest of six places that still lets its reader ask the question cleanly, and moves up only when something forces it.

PlaceWhat changing it costsHolds
Type nameA rename run, and a new version of every facetA different shape, or different time semantics
Definition meta (family, kind)A Definition versionA grouping that crosses concepts, or a note for readers
Payload fieldNothing per fact; the schema may loosen in a new versionWhat varies fact to fact inside one shape
Provenance (source, binding)Fixed when the fact is admittedWho said it
EdgeA fact of its ownA relation between facts
ProjectionA template versionA view, a filter or an ordering

The list runs from dearest to cheapest. Start at the bottom and promote a distinction only for a reason.

What forces a type. A different shape, meaning a different schema. Or different time semantics: a type that closes a series is its own type, because the fold reads the closing rule from the Definition and cannot read it from a payload.

The segments. The concept is the second segment. A third segment is a specialisation, and it means one of two things. It is a kind within the concept (health.medication.started, health.medication.stopped). Or it is the vendor whose own shape the payload is (activity.exercise-session.garmin). A vendor's own payload shape always takes the vendor leaf, even with one vendor. A standard shape (Open mHealth, IEEE 1752) takes none, so the name says whether to expect a portable payload. The Definition's kind is meant to say which of the two a leaf is. Today kind takes measure, reading or event (card 176), so it cannot yet, and the Definition's description has to.

Other rules.

  • What varies fact to fact within one shape is a payload field.
  • Who said it is provenance, unless it changes the shape.
  • family is for a grouping that crosses concepts.
  • A relation between facts is an edge, never a field on either.
  • A domain head is not a distinction a type earns. mind, the domain of self-reported inner states, was added by Matt's decision of 2026-10-22 (card 222), so that mood or energy can join later as concepts without another head. Attention is its first concept: mind.attention is a reading, and mind.attention.started and mind.attention.ended are a pair that closes a series, each of its own type for the reason above.
  • org, the domain of relationships with organisations, was added by Matt's decision of 2026-10-24 (card 239): "I authorise expansion of top level domain org." It does not take work's place, which stays reserved for projects, tasks and output. Employment is its first concept (org.employment.started, org.employment.role-changed and org.employment.ended, a series keyed by org and stint), and membership comes later as org.membership. The cost is accepted: org sorts by counterparty where the other heads sort by subject, so a club could sit in org or social, and a shareholding is money's.
  • money, the domain of what the person paid, was opened from the reserved four by Matt's decision of 2026-10-25 (card 241): "We need a new top level domain, money." The count stays ten, since money was already counted as reserved. Purchases are its first concept (money.purchase, one order or receipt with its lines inside it). The person's judgement about a purchase (deductible, purpose, financial year) is a relation to the facts it is about, so it stays in the note that joins the purchase, and the bank's charged amount and a refund are later facts joined by edges, not fields. A shareholding or a loan would be money too.
  • A type with no live facets and no writer is history. It is marked superseded (card 184), not renamed.

Examples.

  • Exercise sessions split by vendor. A Garmin session and a Concept2 session carry different payload shapes, so each is its own type under activity.exercise-session, not one type with a vendor field. Card 195 split them into four leaves, and renamed activity.day, health.measurement and health.cpap-session to their vendor's leaf.
  • Medication started and stopped as separate types. Stopped closes a series, and the fold reads that from the Definition, so it cannot be a status field on one type.
  • Rowing against walking is a payload field, activity_name. Both are an exercise session of one shape, so a split would only cost a rename run per new sport.
  • Coffee, wine and a rich meal are one type, health.intake, with kind a payload field (card 232). Each is an event at a minute with no series and one time rule, so a split would only cost a type per new kind; the per-kind fields a payload must carry are held by the schema's if and then, and a reader asks for the kind with attr.
  • family: meds is meta. It groups the medication types for a reader and changes no shape, so it needs neither a segment nor a field.

Why. Each place up the list is dearer to change and harder to undo, so the cheap place that works is the right place. A rule that says so gives the same answer each time a new kind of fact arrives, where before it was whatever the last type did.

What it costs. The cheap places are the ones a reader cannot select by in the store, since the store never opens a payload. A distinction that readers will filter on every day has to be argued up, and that argument is a judgement the rule narrows and does not remove. The existing hyphenated names keep their shape until a card moves them; the medication, illness and trip names left theirs in card 196. health.heart-rate-day left its in card 205 by a new type, not a rename: health.heart-rate.day holds a day's three figures as one fact, a different shape, and the old type's facts are retired by the Garmin importer's snapshot.

Where else it is used

  • mentioned by Definition manifest, Attention (the mind domain), Employment (the org domain), Purchase (the money domain)
A hard-coded value is a default or an invariant A value someone could reasonably choose differently is a config key with a shipped default and a range check; a value whose change breaks correctness or a contract stays a constant with a comment saying why; no test hook changes either.

decision supporting basis: code internal/config/limits.go#Limits anchor type Limits struct {

Alternatives

  • Leave each number where it was first needed (the waits, bounds and sizes sat as constants in the daemon, gate, client and inspector, so running Anam differently meant editing code)
  • Make every constant a key (would let someone set MaxBatch below what the importers send, or a token shorter than the floor, and break the contract from a config file)
  • A package variable a test overwrites (refused; a value a test must change is a default, set the way a person would, and one it must not change is an invariant)

Affects Config (internal/config), Daemon, Admission gate, Client (internal/client), Inspector (internal/inspect), Config limits

Tested by internal/config/limits_test.go#TestLimitsOverrideAndRange, internal/config/limits_test.go#TestDefaultLimits

Context. Card 151, from Matt's direction that someone running Anam makes their own choices and Anam ships opinionated defaults. The rule is in AGENTS.md ("Defaults and invariants").

Choice. A default is a snake_case key whose default is a Default… constant in internal/config with a comment saying why that number; Load range-checks it, it reaches the code as a parameter from the one Config loaded at start and is never read from a global or the environment deep in the code, and it is listed in config.example.yaml. An invariant stays a constant with a comment saying why it is not configurable. The invariants card 151 named are gate.MaxBatch (49), the Anam-Timeout header, the snapshot id's shape, clock.Width, the token's 32-character floor, the roles, file modes and the library's folder naming. A store's internal tuning that changes no result a person meets, and a page's geometry, are neither.

Why. The two failure modes differ. A default set badly is a tuning problem and fails at start with a message naming the key. An invariant set differently breaks something the other end of a wire relies on, which no start-up check can see.

What it costs. Every function that used a number now takes it as a parameter (gate.New takes gate.Limits, client.New takes the margin and the response bound, inspect.Serve takes its Deps), and config.example.yaml has 84 more lines to keep true.

Where else it is used

  • decisions A distinction goes in the cheapest place that lets its reader ask cleanly
  • mentioned by Config (internal/config), Config limits
A mint starts from the last run The checkpoints at the last run are the value at that W, so only the buckets the events since then touched can differ; the full mint remains as oracle and repair.

decision supporting basis: docs internal/mint/incremental.go#fromLastRun anchor if d.Budget <= 0 {

Alternatives

  • Fold the whole ledger on every mint and compare each bucket (kept as --full; 19.8 s against 132 ms at 600k facets on the ThinkPad)
  • Decide re-minting by W > covers_w (refused; W is global, so every ingest would re-mint every bucket)

Affects Mint, Checkpoint log, Mint

Tested by internal/mint/incremental_test.go#TestRun_FromTheLastRunIsTheFullMint

Context. Card 62. Whether a bucket needs re-minting is decided by its value, not by bookkeeping about it, and the tail is the pre-filter.

Choice. A run records its W after its checkpoints, and the run records a digest of the checkpoints a read from it takes. The next mint replays (R, W], rebuilds the buckets those events touched from their checkpoint plus the tail, splits over budget, and compares by hash. A log that no longer yields those checkpoints, or holds rows past the run, is one a mint crashed in or a cache lost entries from, so until a mint records a run again mints fold in full and a read that needs a missing checkpoint folds in full.

Why. A mint costs its tail, the buckets it touched and a stat per checkpoint, which is what lets the daemon mint after every change.

What it costs. The digest says the checkpoints are the ones the run took, not that their values are right; the full mint settles every bucket against the fold whatever the log holds, and is the test oracle the incremental path must equal.

Where else it is used

  • mentioned by Mint checkpoints
A person's words are a note, and a model's reading is a draft A conversation is an importer; the person's words are saved at once and verbatim as a record.edge under source conversation, which may name no facet yet; a model's reading is a second source whose facts are drafts the person endorses by a new version.

decision supporting basis: docs docs/API.md#Writing anchor which may name no facet yet (`note.annotation` v3)

Alternatives

  • Store only the words (keeps the rule that a facet holds what its source said, and loses the structure the record is for)
  • Store only the model's facts (gains structure, and attributes the model's reading to the person)
  • Write the person's assertion to a managed records file and let the records importer admit it (the route API.md first designed; overtaken, since a conversation can post envelopes under its own token)

Affects Definition manifest, Importer, Admission gate

Context. Card 93, decided by Matt on 2026-10-15. Capture is where much of the value is, since three of the four stories in docs/compelling-reason-for-Anam.md are writes, but a facet holds what its source said.

Choice. The words are a record.edge under source conversation, written at once. A model may read them into ordinary typed facts under a second source, interpretation; each links to the exact note version it came from and carries standing: draft | endorsed | rejected. The person endorses or rejects a draft with a new version, and nothing is deleted.

What is built. Only the first part: card 157 made note.annotation v3 accept a note that names no facet, so the words can land before any facet exists, and a later version of the same note can name them. Card 104 then made record.note v2 an edge (the type's lineage: note.annotation (v1 to v3), then record.note (v1, v2), now record.edge): from and to as lists of facet ids with a kind, interpreted_from declared for the reading's link back to the note version, and a facet's read answers the live edges touching it beside its payload. The interpretation source, the standing field, endorsement and the inspector's review (cards 158 to 161) are cut but not built (see the interpretation source is decided and not built).

Where else it is used

  • mentioned by The interpretation source is decided and not built
A read may select by payload; the store never does Payload opacity is a storage rule, not a ceiling on what a person may ask of their own record; a query selects and joins by any schema field, read from the served copy through a projection, and the store still never decomposes or indexes a payload.

decision supporting basis: docs docs/ARCHITECTURE.md anchor The **store** never selects by payload; a **read**

Alternatives

  • Limit queries to attributes a Definition declares queryable (the first draft of card 173; refused, since a per-attribute list would be access below projections, which Anam does not have, and the schema already says what every field means)
  • Keep the §8 sentence and answer cross-type questions by hand, as the 2026-10-17 supplement questions were (refused; the joins were being done in an agent's head, which is the thing a checkable query replaces)

Affects Query, Projection, Projections prune, never reshape or generate; a consumer selects, never defines, Metadata and payload are separated; the payload is opaque, Serve (internal/serve)

Context. ARCHITECTURE §8 said "fields narrow what a projection serves, they do not select by payload", on the strength of "no read opens a payload". Read against §3, §8 and §9, that sentence governed storage: the payload is a BLOB, never shredded at ingest, nothing indexed by field. Card 138's fields already read the served copy. On 2026-10-17 the supplement questions ("what was I on, and when wasn't I") were joins across types along fact-time, answered by hand, and card 172's bug was two pipeline steps in the wrong order, which a rule with the bound as a parameter cannot express. Matt asked why Anam would limit what can be queried.

Choice. The store never selects by payload; a read may, over the served copy, through a projection. The query is the third read: a Datalog program over the fold at a watermark, with the envelope and every schema field of the served Definition as relations, bundled rules for Anam's own time semantics, and a receipt that names the projection, rule set and query. §8, §9, §12 and §13 of ARCHITECTURE were amended together on 2026-10-19.

Why. Opacity and queryability were two rules in one sentence. Separating them keeps every storage invariant and removes a ceiling that protected nothing: a projection is the access boundary, and a query that runs through one sees exactly what a timeline would.

What it costs. A second implementation of liveness (current/2 beside fold.Snapshot) that a standing test must hold equal; a query engine that reads whole served payloads, so the read bound and the deadline are the only thing between an agent and a slow answer; and a receipt that grows by the projection hash, the rule-set hash and the query text. Whether fold.Fates later becomes the bundled rules is a separate decision, because it would move the evaluator onto the serve path.

Where else it is used

  • mentioned by Metadata and payload are separated; the payload is opaque, Projections prune, never reshape or generate; a consumer selects, never defines, Query
A token names a principal with a role Every caller is a principal (name, role, token hash); importers keep their source and types, and readers and operators get no source; a route names the roles it admits and the handlers never branch on who is calling.

decision supporting basis: code internal/config/principals.go#LoadPrincipals anchor func LoadPrincipals(path string, bundled func(facet.Type) bool) ([]Principal, error) {

Alternatives

  • Keep two kinds of caller, the operator's one token and per-source importer tokens, and add a wrapper per new kind (how the daemon was until card 89; an assistant that only reads would have needed the operator's token, which also mints, backs up and opens the inspector)
  • Per-route scope lists on each token (finer than anyone needs; the roles are three sets of routes, written once in the route table)
  • A role switch inside each handler (spreads the access rule over every handler instead of one table)

Affects Daemon, Principals and roles (internal/daemon auth), Importers file, anam_submit

Tested by internal/config/principals_test.go#TestLoadPrincipalsWithRoles, internal/daemon/roles_test.go#TestAReaderMayNotHoldTheOperatorsToken

Context. Card 89. A client that reads (an assistant answering from projections) had no token between the operator's, which opens everything, and an importer's, which could read nothing and write one source.

Choice. A principal is a name, a role, the SHA-256 of its token and, for an importer, a source and types. token_file is the operator, held as a hash like the rest. The importers file keeps its importers key, each entry an importer named for its source, and gains a principals key whose entries state their name and role. Names, sources and token hashes are each used once; a principal that is not an importer has no source and no types. The decision for route access is one table (see each route names the roles it admits).

Why. Roles are the floor of access control: the role says which routes a token opens. A reader reads the whole record; narrowing a reader to some projections is later work (see a reader is not narrowed to projections).

What it costs. Adding a principal restarts the daemon, as adding an importer always did. An importer now reads the whole record, which the earlier importer token could not (see the Envelope is the only ingress): the narrowing that remains is that it submits as one source for its types.

Where else it is used

A tombstone is an event, not an artifact version Retraction appends a tombstoned anam-event against the current version and writes no artifact, because an Anam artifact has nowhere to carry a status.

decision supporting basis: docs internal/gate/commit.go#Retract anchor Kind: facet.EventTombstoned, FacetID: facetID, AnamTime: g.clock.Now(),

Alternatives

  • A tombstone as a status-bearing artifact version (impossible here, since status is folded out of the artifact)
  • A delete (refused everywhere; the triggers forbid it)

Affects Admission gate, Ledger store (internal/store/ledger), Tombstone, Current status is derived by the fold, never stored on the artifact

Tested by internal/store/ledger/ledger_test.go#TestMark_TombstoneAdvancesWatermarkWithoutArtifact, internal/gate/gate_test.go#TestCommit_AdmitNoopSupersedeRetract

Context. Anam folds status out of the artifact. A tombstone as a status-bearing artifact version has nowhere to put the status.

Choice. The ledger's primitive is two calls, not one with an optional artifact: AdmitBatch writes artifacts with their events, Mark writes an event alone. The status transition is the whole event. It advances W like any mutation, so a checkpoint cannot reuse a value that still lists a retracted facet.

Why. An optional artifact pointer would make "no artifact, admitted" representable. Keeping the tombstone an event keeps one truth per fact.

What it costs. Nothing visible; a read as of before the tombstone still shows the facet, and the artifact stays forever.

Bucket on fact-time, monthly by default, subdivided by a byte budget Checkpoints partition the timeline by calendar window over fact-time; a bucket over checkpoint_budget splits a year into months and a month into days, so bytes decide when and the calendar decides where.

decision supporting basis: docs internal/span/layout.go#Layout anchor type Layout struct{ split map[string]bool }

Alternatives

  • Bucket on anam-time (not possible here; Anam can bucket on fact-time only because a superseding artifact is the same row re-read, so every version of a facet lands in one bucket)
  • A fixed width only, narrowed by hand as density rises (kept as checkpoint_budget 0; the measured worst bucket was 744 facets, and Garmin's monitoring tables would put 73,000 in one month)

Affects Buckets (internal/span), Mint, Bucket span, Checkpoint log

Tested by internal/mint/read_test.go#TestSubdivision_SplitsByBytesAndReadsAreStillTheFold, internal/mint/mint_test.go#TestRun_FactTimeMovesBetweenBuckets

Context. A re-mint prices the worst bucket, never the mean, and density varies by orders of magnitude across sources.

Choice. Every facet is a member of exactly one bucket by arithmetic on its fact-time. The mint starts from one bucket per year and splits any bucket whose value is over budget (1 MiB default) along the calendar. The layout is derived from the checkpoint log, not stored: a bucket is split once a checkpoint of one of its children exists, and splits only add. Ids are self-describing so old ids keep their meaning when the default changes.

Why. The lifelong view is then a disjoint union of bucket values needing no cross-bucket version resolution, and a checkpoint aligns with the time-bounded question a person asks.

What it costs. A backfilled facet re-opens its sealed bucket. The width is checked once at config load and pinned into the index; changing it is a rebuild of the derived tier, not a migration. Weekly is accepted by config but does not subdivide a year (see weekly width disables composed reads).

Where else it is used

  • change paths Change the bucket width or the checkpoint budget
  • mentioned by Mint checkpoints
Definitions live in the operational store, not the ledger A Definition is how Anam interprets, not what it knew; every version is held in tracking.db, provisioned from the binary or from the importer that registers it, and a registered version is immutable.

decision supporting basis: docs internal/store/ops/ops.sql#registered_definition_no_update anchor registered_definition is immutable

Alternatives

  • Admit each Definition as a fact in the ledger, as Datomic transacts schema (listed as an open decision in IMPORTERS §6, to be decided deliberately, not drifted into)
  • Hold Definitions only in the binary (how it started; importers need to register types the binary does not carry)

Affects Definitions registry (internal/def), Operational store (internal/store/ops), Facet Definition, Register a Definition

Tested by internal/store/ops/ops_test.go#TestRegisteredDefinitions_AreImmutable, internal/def/register_test.go#TestRegister

Context. An artifact's definition_version must still resolve years later, and importers in other repos emit their own types.

Choice. Governance, not record. The definition table holds every bundled version the store has seen; registered_definition holds what importers registered, with triggers refusing update and delete. The live registry is the bundled set plus the newest registered version of each type the binary does not carry. A new version may change schema, classification or series but not identity_key or fact_time, checked when the daemon opens by def.Guard (guardCatalog; persistCatalog stores the catalog's set once every start check has passed (card 257); before card 119 only an ingest ran it) and at registration by def.Register.

Why. The error kernel is smaller for it, and a lost operational store comes back from git and from each importer registering again.

What it costs. Receipts pin only the bundled set's hash, so a registered series rule that later changes is invisible to receipt verify (see receipts do not pin registered Definitions).

Where else it is used

  • change paths Change a Definition's shape with a new version
  • mentioned by Receipts pin the bundled Definition set only, Register a Definition
Every who-may rule is one static policy package internal/policy answers every who-may question as pure Go over the request and the importers file's principals, never a payload; the handlers branch on nothing else about the caller; the flip to a Datalog policy base waits for a stated trigger.

decision supporting basis: code internal/policy/policy.go#Allow anchor func Allow(req Request) Answer {

Alternatives

  • Leave each rule as an if in its handler (how it was; the declared-source rule was walked three times, and the snapshot check and the gate's scopes were one rule in two shapes)
  • Build the policy mesh now, a rule engine over a policy base (Matt, 2026-10-22, "for early personal use it's overkill"; strict leans clinical)
  • Put the policy in the consent gate below the serve boundary (refused; nothing below the handlers knows who is asking, and the consent request carries no principal by design)

Affects Principals and roles (internal/daemon auth), Daemon, Inspector (internal/inspect), CLI (internal/app), Importers file

Tested by internal/policy/doctrine_test.go#TestPolicyReadsNoPayload, internal/policy/policy_test.go#TestAllow, internal/policy/policy_test.go#TestListScope, internal/daemon/receipts_reader_test.go#TestRecordingTokenListsOnlyItsOwnReceipts, internal/daemon/receipts_reader_test.go#TestRecordingTokenVerifiesOnlyItsOwnReceipts

Context. Card 223. About a dozen places in the daemon, the inspector and the CLI branched on who was calling: the role set on each route, any_type, the workbench's allow-list, receipts: always, the receipt-open check, the snapshot report's owner, and the declared-source guard on retraction and retirement. Matt, 2026-10-22: "Free, for early personal use, and it's overkill. Maybe we abstract to a policy go, and keep it static and hard coded, ready for a flip later." A survey (the policy touch-points review of 2026-10-22, in the maintainer's private review notes) found 30 touch points, 17 of them policy.

Choice. internal/policy holds every such rule as a small pure function: Allow for a route's roles, MaySubmitType and ScopesOf (one rule that answers both the handlers and the gate's authenticated pillar), MayRegisterType and MayStoreDocument, Records, TimelineRead, MayOpenReceipt (which gates verify as it gates open), ListScope, Declared, MayRetract and MayRetire for the importers file's sources, and Held, Holdable and MayHold for the admission hold. A handler asks and branches on nothing else about the caller. The route table in the daemon stays the one source for which roles reach a route; Allow is handed the route's roles rather than keeping a copy. The list scope is a table of rows, so a per-principal override later is one more row.

The line it holds. The policy reads the importers file's principals and the request, never a payload. A test walks every package the policy reaches and refuses any that reads a payload, a fold or a projection; it reaches only config, and facet for the name of a type. That keeps access a mechanism and never a judgement of content (see the Envelope is the only ingress for the other side of that boundary). The admission hold is the first rule whose data is operator-authored state that changes while the daemon runs; it stays inside the line because the daemon reads the hold table inside the write and passes the held set in on the request.

The flip, later. The functions can be replaced by the bundled Datalog engine over a policy base (principal/2, flag/2, request/3, and a policy.dl rule set versioned and hashed like anam.dl, so a receipt can name the policy version that admitted the read). The policy base never includes attr/3. The trigger is any of: more than about a dozen rules, a second class of principal (a second person, a clinician's reader with a projection grant), or the first rule that must read beyond the request and the file to answer. Until then the handlers would not change when it flips: they ask the same questions.

What it costs. One more package to read for a rule, and an import test that fails if the policy ever reaches for a package outside the short list, by design.

Where else it is used

  • mentioned by Policy (internal/policy), Principals and roles (internal/daemon auth)
Fact-time is promoted out of the payload at the gate The Definition's time_semantics pointer, Go layout and IANA zone turn a payload field into a first-class UTC column, so the timeline orders and filters without opening a payload.

decision supporting basis: docs internal/def/check.go#FactTimeOf anchor t, err := time.ParseInLocation(tf.Layout, s, loc)

Alternatives

  • Leave fact-time inside the blob and parse every payload on every read (refused; it would make the metadata/payload separation a fiction)
  • Require sources to send UTC (refused; a zoneless source clock is the wall clock where the person stood, so the zone is the Definition's to declare)

Affects Admission gate, Definitions registry (internal/def), Fact-time, Facet artifact row

Context. The timeline is ordered by fact-time and a window is filtered by it; a bucket is arithmetic on it.

Choice. Every Definition declares time_semantics.fact_time = {pointer, layout, zone}. The gate resolves the pointer, parses in the zone, stores UTC in a fixed-width text column on the artifact and every canonical entry. The binary embeds time/tzdata so a container image needs no zone database.

Why. The promotion is what lets every read stay on the governed envelope.

What it costs. A Definition that changes its pointer or layout re-places facets in time, which is why def.Guard refuses a new version that changes it; a new placing rule needs a new ledger from the sources.

Mint cadence follows the ledger The daemon mints after importer batches that moved W, once mint_quiet (2 s) passes with no batch, as its own write on the queue; at start it mints if the last run is behind the head.

decision supporting basis: code internal/daemon/minting.go#mintOnRequest anchor quiet := time.NewTimer(d.cfg.MintQuiet)

Alternatives

  • A size, time or rate trigger (deferred in the first slice; refused in card 61 because a mint from the last run is 60 ms on the NAS, so no trigger earns its complexity)
  • Mint inside the batch's transaction (refused; the mint is not part of what the batch admitted, and its failure must fail nothing)
  • Mint before answering a declared ingest, so a caller waiting on it read from checkpoints with no tail (built, and gone with POST /v1/ingest in card 119)

Affects Daemon, Mint, Admit a batch

Tested by internal/daemon/envelopes_test.go#TestPostEnvelopesMints

Context. Card 61. Freshness comes from the ledger; a mint exists to bound replay, so it should follow the writes rather than a clock.

Choice. One trigger, one mint. An importer batch that changed W requests a mint on a one-slot channel; mintOnRequest waits until no batch has arrived for mint_quiet (2 s by default, a config key since card 151) and then mintAtHead mints once, as its own write. At start, mintIfBehind requests a mint when the last run is behind the head, so a batch admitted just before a shutdown is minted. The operator can also mint by hand, anam checkpoint (POST /v1/checkpoints).

Why. Importers send batches of 49 in bursts; minting after each would cost a rebuild per batch for nothing.

What it costs. A mint that fails after an importer batch is reported only in the daemon log (see mint failure after a batch is only logged).

Where else it is used

  • mentioned by Daemon, Config limits, A mint that fails after an importer batch is reported only in the daemon log
One write worker in the daemon Every ledger and operational-store write inside the daemon is a job on one channel run by one goroutine under the job's deadline; reads use read-only handles and take no turn.

decision supporting basis: code internal/daemon/queue.go#work anchor j.done <- d.run(base, j)

Alternatives

  • Rely on SQLite's file lock and the gate's stale-watermark retry alone (would work for correctness, but a batch and a mint interleaving by lock would make the 503-with-Retry-After contract impossible to state)

Affects Daemon, Catch-up (internal/catchup), Mint, Queue wait spends the caller's budget up to acceptance

Tested by internal/daemon/daemon_test.go#TestWritesRunOneAtATime, internal/daemon/daemon_test.go#TestCallerGivingUpDoesNotCutTheWrite

Context. Cards 42 to 45 made the daemon the one process that owns the files.

Choice. Operator writes wait accept_wait (250 ms) to be accepted; an importer's batch waits importer_wait (5 s), long enough to wait out a mint and short of a long write such as a backup, and a busy answer asks the caller to retry after retry_after (15 s). The wait spends the caller's budget (card 110; see queue wait spends the caller's budget). An accepted write runs on the daemon's context, so a client that disconnects does not cut it short and an answer never misreports a write that happened. The mint after importer batches is its own job, so its failure fails no batch.

Why. Serialising writes makes catch-up and the mint simple: each sees the ledger it was queued behind.

What it costs. A ledger lock held by another process (a CLI run against the same files) surfaces as a 500, not a 503 (see ledger busy is not a 503); the exact wait numbers are config defaults with the reason beside each in internal/config/limits.go (see config limits).

Where else it is used

  • invariants Queue wait spends the caller's budget up to acceptance
  • mentioned by Config limits, Queue wait spends the caller's budget up to acceptance
Per-source importer tokens are the authenticated pillar Each importer has its own token; the daemon holds only its SHA-256 beside its source and types; the token names the source, opens the write routes, and an importer reads as a reader does.

decision supporting basis: docs internal/daemon/auth.go#who anchor if who, ok := d.principals[hashOf(p)]; ok {

Alternatives

  • One static source-to-types map in the binary (how mvpScopes worked for the in-process workers until card 119; replaced for importers in card 53 because a push source needs real credentials)
  • Token rotation and multi-scope tokens (deferred, ARCHITECTURE §13)

Affects Daemon, Admission gate, Importers file, Principals and roles (internal/daemon auth), anam_submit

Tested by internal/daemon/envelopes_test.go#TestImporterRoutesAuthorize, internal/daemon/envelopes_test.go#TestOpenRefusesAnImporterWithTheOperatorsToken

Context. Splunk's HTTP Event Collector pins a token to its sourcetypes; the same shape makes the gate's second pillar credentials rather than a constant.

Choice. The importers file is read once at start. A request's bearer is hashed and looked up, and the principal it finds must hold a role the route admits (see a token names a principal with a role): the operator's token is a 403 on the submit routes, and an importer's is a 403 on minting, backups, projection templates and the inspector. The importer's types become the gate's scopes for that batch and the only types it may register. The token lives in the gateway's vault, granted to one anam_submit instance.

Why. An importer cannot write another source's facets, and the daemon never holds a plaintext importer token. Since card 89 an importer can read the record, as a reader does, and sees only its own source's snapshots.

What it costs. Adding an importer restarts the daemon; the token_scope table meant for this is unused (see token_scope is a dead table).

Publication is not an anam-event Serving changes nothing Anam knows, so there is no published event; a receipt records what was shown, and a facet's published time is the earliest served_at over the receipts naming it.

decision supporting basis: docs internal/serve/serve.go#Publish anchor if _, err := d.Published.Put(ctx, receipt.ServedKind, servedBytes); err != nil {

Alternatives

  • A sixth anam-event kind, published (refused; it would braid what Anam knows with what Anam has shown, the same complecting the ledger-versus-canonical split untangles)

Affects Serve (internal/serve), Receipt (internal/receipt), Receipt, Anam-event

Tested by internal/serve/serve_test.go#TestRun_LeavesTheLedgerUntouched

Context. Every remaining event kind maps one-to-one onto a derivable status; publication would not.

Choice. Serving writes two content-addressed rows of the operational store, a served value and a receipt, and nothing in the ledger. The receipt pins the served hash, the facet-id set, the watermark and the ledger it counts in, the template and definition-set versions, served_at, the span and the narrowing query.

Why. One word, one surface: the ledger is what Anam knew, receipts are what it showed.

What it costs. Published time moves out of the ledger into the operational store's authored tier, which is backed up, not rebuilt: lose the receipts and published time is gone. There is no separate receipt index; listing reads the receipt table and sorts by served_at.

Retraction by complete resend An importer never tombstones; it labels a resend-everything run as the parts of one snapshot, Anam reports what is live but not carried, and the operator retracts with guards.

decision supporting basis: docs internal/daemon/snapshots.go#partDigest anchor return "sha256:" + hex.EncodeToString(h.Sum(nil))

Alternatives

  • Let an importer send tombstones (refused; a tombstone cannot be undone by a resend, and an importer is outside the error kernel)
  • Let an ingest tombstone what a scan did not return (refused; a row missing from one scan and a source gone look alike from inside an envelope)

Affects Daemon, Operational store (internal/store/ops), anam_submit, Retract from a snapshot, Snapshot (resend)

Tested by internal/daemon/snapshots_test.go#TestSnapshotLeavesAloneWhatLandedWhileItRan, internal/daemon/snapshots_bind_test.go#TestSnapshotPartIsBoundToItsEnvelopes, internal/app/snapshot_retract_internal_test.go#TestRetract_PlansAnImportersSourceFromItsSnapshot

Context. Cards 78, 94, 107, 120. With push importers, Anam no longer scans a source and cannot see what it no longer holds.

Choice. A resend labels each batch {id, part, parts, types}. Anam keeps the parts and the facet ids they carried (no-ops included) in disposable tables, binds each part to the digest of its envelopes so a part resent with other contents is a 409, and once every part has arrived reports absent as live, of the run's types, not carried, and untouched since the run's first part. anam retract <source> plans from the newest complete snapshot, refuses an empty run, bounds a run at a tenth by default, and writes nothing without --yes. A source replaced under another name is retired whole with --retired --yes --max-fraction 1, after its declaration is removed.

Why. The one gap Anam cannot see is the importer's own, between reading its source and sending the first part; everything else is judged from the watermark.

What it costs. A crash between the ledger commit and recording the part leaves the part unrecorded, so a changed resend after such a crash is admitted as the part; the protocol accepts this. The alert reaches the operator through a ductile event; Anam sends nothing itself.

Where else it is used

  • mentioned by Resend a source whole, Retract from a snapshot
The gate composes identity from the Definition An envelope may leave source_ref out; the gate composes it from identity_key through one length-prefixed function and refuses one that disagrees, so importers shape payloads and nothing else.

decision supporting basis: docs internal/def/check.go#SourceRef anchor return compose("identity", source, d.IdentityKey, doc)

Alternatives

  • Each capture worker composes its own source_ref (how the in-binary vendor workers drifted; the last in-binary workers went in card 119)
  • Join the identity values with a plain separator (refused; a value carrying the separator, a timestamp's colons, could make two tuples one string)

Affects Admission gate, Definitions registry (internal/def), Importer base (anam_importer), Source ref

Tested by internal/gate/gate_test.go#TestCommit_ComposesASourceRefLeftOut, internal/facet/id_test.go#TestSourceRef_DistinctTuplesNeverCollide

Context. Card 51, the first prerequisite for importers in any language without importing Anam's code. The identity rule has to live once.

Choice. facet.SourceRef writes the source name then each identity value as <bytes>:<value>. The gate resolves the Definition's identity_key pointers against the decoded payload, in declared order, and composes it. An envelope that carries a different one is ErrIdentityMismatch. The facet id is uuidv5 of the result under a fixed namespace.

Why. Identity decides which fact; the content hash decides whether it changed. Braiding values into the key would give a corrected reading a new facet id, orphaning the original instead of superseding it, and silently disabling idempotency for that source.

What it costs. An importer still writes the payload fields the pointers name (bp_csv's ordinal reading, garmin's id), which is where "compose no identity" has an edge the contract does not settle (see does writing an id field compose identity).

Where else it is used

  • mentioned by Whether writing an id field counts as composing identity is not settled, Admit a batch
The importer's client stays on its origin and trusts only a whole receipt anam_submit reports a batch admitted only when the 200 body is a complete admission receipt; no redirect is followed, one deadline bounds the whole call, answers are capped at 8 MiB, and a cut answer is retryable.

decision supporting basis: history ../anam-importers/anam_importer/client.py#_call anchor if 300 <= resp.status < 400:

Alternatives

  • urllib with redirects followed and any 200 taken as success (how it was before cards 112 to 117; a redirect would carry the bearer token to another host, and a truncated 200 looked like success)

Affects Importer base (anam_importer), anam_submit, Admission response

Context. Commit 58e692d in anam-importers, cards 112 to 117: "the client stays on its origin and trusts only a whole receipt".

Choice. http.client instead of urllib; a 3xx is a non-retryable error; admission checks w, changed and one result per envelope with a known outcome before anam.admitted is emitted; IncompleteRead and transport errors are retryable; the timeout is one deadline over the whole call with a sliding socket timeout and a size cap; a request that is JSON but not an object gets a structured error.

Why. The gateway retries on the plugin's word; a wrong "admitted" would never be resent, and a token that follows a redirect leaves its origin.

What it costs. The client accepts tombstoned as an outcome the contract never documents; a 409 is just a non-retryable error with Anam's message.

The ledger is its own file, apart from the operational store Two SQLite files, no transaction across them; the derived tier catches up by replay and is behind after a crash, never wrong.

decision supporting basis: docs internal/catchup/catchup.go#Sync anchor if err := o.PinLedger(ctx, id); err != nil {

Alternatives

  • One database holding the ledger and its indexes, written in one transaction (refused; then deleting the index could reach a fact, and nothing would prove the derived tier derived)
  • A ledger with its own secondary indexes (refused; an index is derived, and derived things live in the operational store)

Affects Ledger store (internal/store/ledger), Operational store (internal/store/ops), Catch-up (internal/catchup), Operational store

Tested by internal/catchup/catchup_test.go#TestSync_RefusesACursorAheadOfTheLedger, internal/store/ops/derived_test.go#TestApplyReplay_MovesRowsAndCursorTogether

Context. The file is the unit SQLite deletes, locks and opens read-only by.

Choice. The ledger holds artifacts, events and pins, nothing else, and serves two access paths on primary keys. Everything a reader wants beyond that is an index in tracking.db, rebuilt by replaying events since the cursor. The operational store pins the ledger's id and refuses an index of another ledger.

Why. The repair of the operational store is to delete its file and replay, and that repair cannot reach a fact. Deleting it and replaying is also how the rebuild test proves the derived tier derived.

What it costs. The index is never written in the same breath as the ledger: a crash leaves it behind, and anam retract leaves it behind on purpose until the daemon's next start. Catch-up has to move its cursor conditionally in the same transaction as its rows.

Where else it is used

  • mentioned by Rebuild the record from its sources
The person is the substrate The person, not the episode, is the unit of storage, and the whole-life read is the default; minting stays per bucket.

decision supporting basis: docs docs/ARCHITECTURE.md#The whole-person read anchor ### The whole-person read (read this before anything else)

Alternatives

  • Scope every read to an episode, where every read is scoped to a bounded episode and the person is a thin identity index (refused, because a personal record wants to be longitudinal)
  • Make the whole timeline the mint unit as well as the read default (tried, ruled out in ARCHITECTURE §1 as a conflation of read default and mint unit)

Affects Read (internal/read), Mint, The whole-person read, Derived span

Context. A person is unbounded and "longitudinal is system-of-record gravity". A personal soul-record wants exactly that gravity.

Choice. Re-promote the person. A ledger file is pinned to one person_id, every facet is on one timeline in fact-time order, and a read is "my facts, in time order", optionally narrowed. No identity graph, no merge.

Why. At the scale of one life the cost of that gravity is affordable, and the longitudinal account is the whole product.

What it costs. Anam owns the continuity: the composed, ordered account exists nowhere upstream. It is re-derivable by re-ingesting every source, but not held anywhere else while Anam is off, so the ledger's order, watermarks and checkpoints are backed up rather than assumed rebuildable. This does not license whole-life minting: the read default is unbounded, the mint unit is a bucket.

The substrate is accidental: only internal/store names SQLite The problem is a personal record, not a store; scripts/check.sh fails the build if database/sql or sqlite is named outside internal/store, with no exception.

decision supporting basis: docs scripts/check.sh#boundary anchor grep -vE '^github.com/mattjoyce/anam/internal/store'

Alternatives

  • Let the domain packages query SQL directly (refused; the domain must not know what it is stored in, and the ledger asks only for ordered keys and one atomic multi-row write)
  • Let internal/capture name database/sql to read a source that is a SQLite file (held until card 119, as the source's substrate rather than Anam's; dropped when the last in-process worker moved out to an importer)

Affects Ledger store (internal/store/ledger), Operational store (internal/store/ops), Facet domain values (internal/facet)

Context. AGENTS.md's design lens: Hickey on what is complected. The ledger moved from DynamoDB to SQLite once already (journal, 2026-09-28) behind the same interface.

Choice. The row mapping is quarantined to the store package; a row is not a domain type. There is no carve-out: since card 119 no package in the binary reads a source, so a source that is a SQLite file is read by its importer.

Why. Because Anam owns no source fact, a change of substrate can be a re-ingest; the anam-times are what would not come back, so a ledger whose history matters is moved by copying rows.

What it costs. A mechanical grep rather than a type-level guarantee; and the words "store" and "database" are easy to say in a package that should not.

Where else it is used

  • mentioned by Only internal/store may name SQLite or database/sql
Two clocks, not four A facet carries fact-time and nothing else; an anam-event carries anam-time and nothing else; recorded-time and ingested-time were deleted as duplicates.

decision supporting basis: docs internal/clock/clock.go#Layout anchor const Layout = "2006-01-02T15:04:05.000000000Z07:00"

Alternatives

  • {'Four clocks, with a recorded time (when the source documented it) and an ingested time (when Anam received it) on the facet (built, then deleted': "the first was a duplicate column across 893 rows, the second a second home for the admitted event's anam-time)"}
  • A published time as a third clock on the ledger (refused; it is derived from receipts)

Affects Fact-time, Anam-time, Facet artifact row, Anam-event row

Context. Telling "when was this true in the world" from "when did Anam learn it" is the difference between history and audit.

Choice. The split is structural, not conventional. A facet has one clock, fact-time, read from the payload by the Definition's pointer; an anam-event has one clock, anam-time, stamped by the gate. Neither can borrow the other's because neither has a field for it. Times are text in one fixed-width layout, so they compare lexically in chronological order; time.RFC3339Nano is explicitly refused because it elides trailing zeros.

Why. Every extra clock was a second place for the same answer to disagree from. Where the facet page once read ingested time off the artifact, it now reads it off the event that admitted that version.

What it costs. Anam-time has resolution but a bulk backfill compresses its span, so an as-of inside an import returns a read-order artefact. That is honest: Anam did not exist then. ARCHITECTURE §8 still calls the layout RFC3339Nano, which the code refuses (see architecture doc names the wrong time layout).

Commands that only read open the ledger read-only Every read command and every daemon read route uses a mode=ro handle, so SQLite itself refuses a write; only init, retract --yes and the daemon open it read-write.

decision detail basis: code internal/store/ledger/ledger.go#OpenReadOnly anchor return openExisting(ctx, path, personID, modeReadOnly)

Alternatives

  • One read-write handle shared by every command (refused; a reader that can write is a refactor away from writing)

Affects Ledger store (internal/store/ledger), CLI (internal/app), Daemon

Tested by internal/store/ledger/open_test.go#TestOpenReadOnly_RefusesEveryWrite, internal/store/ledger/open_test.go#TestOpenReadOnly_IsNotBlockedByAnOpenWrite

Context. The threat the triggers answer is a refactor or a hurried session, not an adversary; a read-only handle is the same answer one layer down.

Choice. OpenReadOnly opens with mode=ro and skips index creation; the daemon keeps one for every read route, the inspector and /healthz. The write handle's DSN adds WAL, synchronous FULL and an immediate transaction lock.

Why. A read-only open is also not blocked by an open writer, so reads proceed while a batch commits.

What it costs. ARCHITECTURE §8 still says only init and ingest write, which stopped being true when the daemon and retract arrived and ingest went in card 119; the comment on openLedgerReadOnly now names init, the daemon and retract (see architecture doc has stale sentences). The operational store is opened read-write by local read commands, so a local anam timeline creates tracking.db.

decision detail basis: docs internal/def/manifests/health.blood-pressure.json#required anchor "required": ["date_excel", "reading", "systolic_blood_pressure", "diastolic_blood_pressure", "effective_time_frame", "source_time"],

Card 195 split it into a leaf per vendor, since each fills its own quarter of the fields beside that body: the body and a shared core stay on every leaf.

Choice. A pinned OMH schema inside Anam's closed JSON Schema, plus the export's own columns as text so nothing the source wrote is lost. Identity and fact-time still come from the source's fields (date_excel, reading).

Why. An existing vocabulary lets sessions and readings from different sources sit on one timeline as one type.

What it costs. Each new source re-shapes to the vocabulary rather than passing its rows through, and the extractors that wrote v4 rows still say v4 while the current version is v5.

Published reads page by a checked cursor A published read over max_read_bytes is filed once and answered a page at a time, each page cut from the served value; the cursor names its format, the receipt, the offset and the facet there, and is checked against the receipt, not signed.

decision detail basis: code internal/read/page.go#decodeCursor anchor case ids[c.Offset] != c.FacetID:

Alternatives

  • Refuse with 413 and ask the reader to narrow (card 88, how it was; an answer that needs every facet had no way to be read over the daemon)
  • Page by re-reading the head at an offset (each page a new read, so pages could disagree with each other and with the receipt)
  • A cursor signed with a daemon key (a key to keep and rotate, for a check the immutable receipt already makes)
  • A /pages sub-route (the first page would be answered in two places)

Affects Read (internal/read), Daemon, Client (internal/client), Reads over the daemon are bounded by bytes

Tested by internal/daemon/page_test.go#TestReceiptPages_WalkTheWholePublishedRead, internal/read/page_internal_test.go#TestDecodeCursor, internal/app/bound_internal_test.go#TestPublish_OverTheBoundPublishesOnceAndPages

Context. Card 92, on cards 87 (receipted reads), 88 (the byte bound) and 91 (one facet by its route).

Choice. POST /v1/receipts files the whole value and its receipt once and answers the first page; GET /v1/receipts/{hash}?cursor= reads each later page, and without a cursor the first. A page is the most whole facets from its offset that fit max_read_bytes by Fit's measure, cut by ReceiptPage from the served value, the first page included. The cursor is base64url of its format, the receipt hash, the offset and the facet id at that offset; a cursor that does not match the receipt it is presented to is 400 cursor_invalid. The receipt rides the first page only. 413 is left for one facet over the bound alone, naming it, with the cursor past it on a page.

Why. A page is a portion of a receipted value, never a new read of the head, so every page belongs to the same answer whatever Anam learns between pages. The receipt is immutable and content-addressed, so its own facet_ids are the cursor's check and no key is needed.

What it costs. Each page re-reads and parses the whole served file. The receipt's facet_ids sit outside the measure on the first page, as every answer's header does.

Where else it is used

  • decisions Reads over the daemon are bounded by bytes
Reads over the daemon are bounded by bytes A timeline over max_read_bytes is refused with 413 and the parameters that would narrow it, measured on the wire encoding; a published read pages instead (card 92); a local read and a verify are never bounded.

decision detail basis: code internal/read/bound.go#Fit anchor if n > limit {

Alternatives

  • A row limit with a cursor (refused for changes_since, where a non-unique anam_time would step over a batch's instant; a byte bound names the cost the daemon actually pays)
  • No bound (how it was; a whole-person read over HTTP could take the daemon's memory and the client's)

Affects Read (internal/read), Daemon, Client (internal/client), Timeline response, Published reads page by a checked cursor

Tested by internal/app/bound_internal_test.go#TestTimeline_OverTheBoundIsRefusedWithWhatToNarrowBy, internal/app/bound_internal_test.go#TestPublish_OneFacetOverTheBoundFilesNothing

Context. Card 88.

Choice. Fit encodes entries exactly as they will be sent and stops at the first one crossing max_read_bytes (512 KiB default, 0 unbounded). The refusal says how many matched, the bytes, the limit, and which of type, from, to, at, projection were unused. Since card 92 a publish or a receipt show over the bound is answered a page at a time instead (see published reads page by a checked cursor); only one facet over the bound alone is refused, and a publish holding one files nothing. The CLI client separately refuses a body over 64 MiB.

Why. A local read is the operator at their own files; over the daemon the answer travels and is held twice.

What it costs. The daemon computes the whole value before measuring it; a verify re-derives without a bound because it is the proof.

Where else it is used

  • decisions Published reads page by a checked cursor
  • change paths Add a read command that works locally and over the daemon
  • mentioned by Read the timeline
Stored content is a row keyed by the SHA-256 of its bytes A checkpoint value, served value, receipt or saved selection is a row of the operational store keyed by the hash of its own bytes; a put of equal bytes is idempotent, different bytes at one key are corruption, and every read re-hashes.

decision detail basis: code internal/store/ops/content.go#Put anchor res, err := d.db.ExecContext(ctx, t.put, digest, b)

Alternatives

  • Files under an artifact directory, named by the hash and written by temp file and rename (the design until card 215, when the ledger was in DynamoDB and a content-addressed value fitted neither store; since the ledger moved to SQLite it left a second thing to back up and a folder that was never collected)

Affects Operational store (internal/store/ops), Checkpoint value row, Receipt

Tested by internal/store/ops/content_test.go#TestPut_ConcurrentWritersAndReadersSeeWholeRows, internal/store/ops/content_test.go#TestPut_RefusesDifferentBytesAtTheSameKey, internal/store/ops/content_test.go#TestTriggers_RefuseWhatImmutableMeans

Context. Card 215. The key is a promise about its bytes, as the file name was.

Choice. Six tables of one shape (the sixth, inline_projection, is card 211's: the bytes a receipt's template hash names, keyed by that hash, first for a projection a query carried and since card 257 for every receipt's template), a hash and the bytes it names (checkpoint_value derived; receipt, served_value, saved_selection, saved_selection_code, inline_projection authored). Put inserts and, if the key is there, compares the bytes and returns the digest or ErrCorrupt; Get re-hashes on read. The authored five refuse an update and a delete by trigger; checkpoint_value, a cache, refuses an update and lets a mint delete a value it no longer reads. Value before receipt when publishing, so a crash leaves a value no receipt names and never the reverse.

Why. Content addressing only means something if the bytes under a key are whole, and a backup is only one consistent thing if the receipts are in the same file as everything else Anam authored.

What it costs. A schema bump, so a store from before is rebuilt and the folder moved in (anam move-artifacts). The triggers refuse an update and a delete but not an insert that replaces, as in the registered tables; the store's own writes never replace.

Where else it is used

  • mentioned by Operational store (internal/store/ops), Publish a projection

Invariants (29)

Anam owns no source fact No captured payload exists only inside Anam; every one is verbatim from its source and rebuildable, so deleting Anam loses no fact. Anam does own the continuity.

invariant core basis: docs docs/ARCHITECTURE.md#Purpose and boundary anchor > **Anam owns no source fact, and no captured payload exists only inside it

Applies to Admission gate, Ledger store (internal/store/ledger), Facet

Enforced at not enforced

Tested by internal/fold/rebuild_test.go#TestRebuildFromSource_IdenticalCanonical

The one invariant every other decision serves (ARCHITECTURE §1 and §12). If Anam were deleted tomorrow, every payload rebuilds from its source: the gate holds the payload opaque, the ledger stores it as the bytes it arrived as, and nothing in Anam ever authors a source fact.

What is not rebuildable is named just as plainly: the continuity. The composed, ordered, whole-life account exists nowhere upstream, so the ledger's order, its watermarks and its checkpoints are backed up rather than assumed rebuildable. The honest sentence is "Anam owns no fact, and Anam owns the continuity."

There is no mechanism that enforces this; it is a property of never parsing a payload and never writing back. The rebuild test is the check: re-ingesting the sources into a fresh ledger reaches the same canonical value.

commit is idempotent and atomic Artifact, anam-event and watermark land in one transaction in one file; same identity and same hash is a no-op, a different hash supersedes, and a refused batch writes nothing, so a worker may crash mid-batch and be re-run to known-good.

invariant core basis: code internal/store/ledger/write.go#write anchor if h != readW {

Applies to Admission gate, Ledger store (internal/store/ledger), Error kernel, Idempotency

Enforced at internal/store/ledger/write.go#write (anchor if h != readW {)

Tested by internal/gate/gate_test.go#TestCommit_CrashRerunIsANoOp, internal/gate/batch_test.go#TestCommitBatch_IsIdempotent, internal/store/ledger/batch_test.go#TestAdmitBatch_StaleWatermarkWritesNothing, internal/gate/gate_test.go#TestCommit_ConcurrentWritersBothLand

ARCHITECTURE §12, eleventh bullet, second half, and §8. Inside the transaction, in order: the head is the watermark the caller read, else ErrStaleWatermark and the gate re-reads, re-stamps anam-time and retries; this (facet_id, version) is not already written, else same bytes is ErrAlreadyWritten (our own earlier write, a no-op) and different bytes is ErrDivergentArtifact (a lost update, stop); the database accepts the insert, else ErrCorrupt. Collapsing the first two into one conflict would either retry a write that succeeded or reject a benign re-ingest, so the mapping is the idempotency, not error handling around it.

The retry loop belongs to the gate, not the store, and there is no transaction across the two files. This, plus the triggers, is the error kernel.

Current status is derived by the fold, never stored on the artifact A facet carries its birth status and nothing else; admitted, superseded, tombstoned, endorsed and rejected are folded from anam-events on every read.

invariant core basis: code internal/fold/fold.go#Apply anchor abbreviated to facet.Status(ev.Kind): that conversion compiles

ARCHITECTURE §12, sixth bullet: no place-oriented programming. The facet table has no status column and the Go struct no status field, pinned by a test that reflects over it. A tombstone is therefore an anam-event, not an artifact version, because there is nowhere on an artifact to put one.

The fold is the only derivation, and it is spelled out kind by kind rather than converting an event kind to a status, so that a new kind cannot silently become a status. The operational index persists a folded state for speed, but it is derived tier: replaying the ledger remakes it.

An importer never tombstones; the operator retracts There is no automatic retraction: an importer reports what its source still holds through a snapshot, and only anam retract, with guards and --yes, writes a tombstone.

Enforced at not enforced

docs/IMPORTERS.md §8.6; Matt, 2026-10-10. The daemon offers no route that tombstones, so the invariant holds by the absence of an ingress rather than by a check: Mark is reachable only from gate.Retract, which only anam retract and the tests call. A tombstone cannot be undone (a resend of the same row is a no-op), and an importer is outside the error kernel.

ARCHITECTURE §7: a single indexed range scan. The current canonical is the head case; an as-of is the same scan with an upper bound. Where else it is used

  • mentioned by Read the timeline, Rebuild the record from its sources
Append-only is enforced by the ledger's triggers, for every writer Facts are added and re-statused, never modified, never deleted; triggers on every ledger table refuse UPDATE, DELETE and an INSERT over an existing key, binding a sqlite3 shell as much as Anam.

invariant supporting basis: code internal/store/ledger/ledger.sql#facet_no_update anchor CREATE TRIGGER IF NOT EXISTS facet_no_update BEFORE UPDATE ON facet

Applies to Ledger store (internal/store/ledger), Ledger, Supersession, Tombstone

Enforced at internal/store/ledger/ledger.sql#facet_no_update (anchor CREATE TRIGGER IF NOT EXISTS facet_no_replace BEFORE INSERT ON facet)

Tested by internal/store/ledger/appendonly_test.go#TestAppendOnly_DatabaseRefusesEveryOverwrite, internal/store/ledger/appendonly_test.go#TestAppendOnly_ABackupKeepsTheTriggers

ARCHITECTURE §12, fifth bullet, and AGENTS.md: never add a code path that drops or disables a ledger trigger. Three triggers per table: no update, no delete, and no insert over an existing key, the last because INSERT OR REPLACE would otherwise overwrite a row without firing the delete trigger. Two more keep the structure sound: an artifact version n needs n-1 beneath it, and an event's seq must be exactly MAX(seq)+1.

A trigger is not a security boundary: whoever holds the file can drop it. The threat it answers is a refactor or a hurried session, not an adversary. Ledger.Drop exists for the rebuild test only.

Canonical means the live-only value; the ledger means append-everything-marked One word, one surface: the canonical is the fold's live facets at a watermark, superseded and tombstoned ones excluded from the value and retained in the ledger, marked.

invariant supporting basis: code internal/fold/fold.go#Live anchor return s.Status == facet.StatusAdmitted || s.Status == facet.StatusEndorsed

Applies to Fold, Canonical, Ledger, Projection

Tested by internal/fold/fold_test.go#TestFold_Liveness

ARCHITECTURE §12, ninth bullet. Anam once used "canonical" for the append-everything surface; that is corrected. A projection over the canonical sees live facets by construction, which is why tombstone visibility is no longer a projection knob: a reader who wants tombstones is asking the ledger a forensic question.

invariant supporting basis: code internal/daemon/api.go#routes anchor importerOnly = []config.Role{config.RoleImporter}

Applies to Daemon, Principals and roles (internal/daemon auth), Importers file

Enforced at internal/policy/policy.go#Allow (anchor case !slices.Contains(req.Roles, req.Caller.Role):)

Tested by internal/daemon/roles_test.go#TestEveryRouteAdmitsTheRolesItNames, internal/daemon/roles_test.go#TestAReaderWritesNothingButReceipts, internal/policy/policy_test.go#TestAllow

Card 89. routes() returns every route but /healthz with its roles, and Handler mounts each behind admit. Reading the record and publishing a read are every role's. Submitting is an importer's alone, because an envelope is admitted as its importer's source and the operator is no source. Running the daemon is the operator's, and so is the inspector, which shows the whole record unprojected. Before card 89 this was three wrapper functions (authorize, asImporter, asReader) and the inspector sat under the operator's token by being mounted last; a new route had to pick one by remembering to.

admit hands the route's roles to policy.Allow (card 223), which keeps no copy of the table: the table stays the one source, mirrored in openapi.yaml's x-anam-roles. The table's constructor takes the roles as an argument, so a route cannot be added without naming them, and the test sends a token of each role to every route in the table and checks it is admitted exactly when the route names its role. The roles are an invariant, not a config key: each is a set of routes this build has, and a role the build does not know would open nothing it can name.

Where else it is used

  • change paths Add a read command that works locally and over the daemon
  • mentioned by Add a read command that works locally and over the daemon, Daemon, Principals and roles (internal/daemon auth), A token names a principal with a role, The Envelope is the only ingress, Role
Metadata and payload are separated; the payload is opaque Anam governs and projections filter on the facet's attributes and descriptor; the payload is verbatim, immutable, stored as bytes and never parsed into a canonical schema.

invariant supporting basis: code internal/store/ledger/ledger.sql#facet anchor payload BLOB NOT NULL,

Applies to Ledger store (internal/store/ledger), Projection, Admission gate, Facet

Enforced at internal/store/ledger/ledger.sql#facet (anchor payload BLOB NOT NULL,)

ARCHITECTURE §12, tenth bullet, and §3. The payload column is a BLOB, never a JSON column. The gate reads a payload to validate it, to extract fact-time, series and identity, and to canonicalise it for the hash, and writes it unaltered: a number the source wrote as text stays text. A projection's verdict reads fold.Entry fields only, and the consent request has no payload field by design. The ledger is datom-shaped with a fat opaque value, not EAVT: there is no systolic attribute in the store to ask for, and nothing is indexed by field. This is a storage rule, not a ceiling on reads: fields, and the query card 173 decided, read payload fields from the served copy after a projection has decided what is served (a read may select by payload; the store never does).

ARCHITECTURE §12, third bullet, second half. The read default is unbounded and the mint unit is bounded, and the two must not be conflated: Anam once reasoned "no required span, therefore the mint unit is the whole timeline"; it does not hold. settle only ever hashes and writes one bucket's entries; there is no code path that writes a whole-person checkpoint.

Projections prune, never reshape or generate; a consumer selects, never defines A template includes or excludes whole facets by metadata filters only, and the only templates are the versioned ones bundled in the binary.

invariant supporting basis: code internal/projection/projection.go#Apply anchor verdicts, err := Explain(v, t, g, s)

Applies to Projection, Serve (internal/serve), Projection, Query, Projection template

Enforced at internal/projection/projection.go#Apply (anchor verdicts, err := Explain(v, t, g, s))

Tested by internal/projection/projection_test.go#TestApply_PropertyPruneOnly, internal/projection/projection_test.go#TestApply_TypeAllowListDeniesByDefault

ARCHITECTURE §12, twelfth bullet, and §9. The knobs are a type allow-list (deny by default), a status filter, a maximum classification, a fact-time window and a shape; the shape is the one knob whose verdict depends on other entries, which is why it is a template knob and not a read-time flag. A served value is a subset of the canonical's entries with the same bytes per entry, which the property test checks over random templates. A wide-open template reproduces the canonical hash.

A query does generate, and this invariant survives it because the query runs over what the projection served, never beside it: the projection still only prunes, the query relates the facets it admitted, and the answer is a new value with its own receipt, not a change to any facet (a read may select by payload).
